(76)

is the final functional, since no further spurious contributions are produced when evaluated for the maximal cavities.
Defining the weighted densities

n_{5}(\boldsymbol{s}):=\rho_{\text{pc},x}(\boldsymbol{s}),\qquad n_{6}(\boldsymbol{s}):=\rho_{\text{pc},y}(\boldsymbol{s}),\qquad n_{7}(\boldsymbol{s}):=\rho_{\text{c}}(\boldsymbol{s})\qquad,

(77)

the excess free energy density in
\beta\tilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\text{AO}}^{\text{ex}}=\sum_{\boldsymbol{s}}\Phi_{\text{AO,2D}}(\boldsymbol{s})
is given by

\Phi_{\text{AO,2D}}(\boldsymbol{s})=\Phi^{\text{0D}}\left(n_{1}\right)+\Phi^{\text{0D}}\left(n_{2}\right)+\Phi^{\text{0D}}\left(n_{3}\right)+\Phi^{\text{0D}}\left(n_{4}\right)\\
-\Phi^{\text{0D}}\left(n_{5}\right)-\Phi^{\text{0D}}\left(n_{6}\right)-3\,\Phi^{\text{0D}}\left(n_{7}\right)\qquad.

(78)

Figure 11
:
Visualization of the hard interactions in the mixture of colloids (green cube) and
polymer clusters (of type pc
,y
) in 3D.
The polymer cluster pc
,y
consist of two pyramids (upper left part) with a square base glued together
on their side surfaces. One of the pyramids has a hemispherical cutout in the center of its base and the other a
hemispherical bulge (as shown). The bulge forbids a colloid (green cube) to sit next to the cluster
in the direction of the bulge. The other two polymer clusters pc
,x
and pc
,z
look same in shape
but differ in their orientation. (The right orientation can be obatined by permutig the coordinate
axes:
(xyz)\to(zxy)
for pc
,x
and
(xyz)\to(yzx)
for pc
,z
.)
For polymer clusters of different species, the hemisphere fits exactly into the cutout so that the
clusters can sit side by side, and the pyramid geometry of the clusters enables them to simultaneously sit
on the same lattice site.
Maximal zero-dimensional cavities (right): The cavities 1, 3 and 5 differ only in the direction the bulge points.
They can either host a colloid or a certain polymer cluster at the lattice site
\boldsymbol{s}
.
The cavities 2, 4 and 6 of figure (b) also differ only in their orientation and can either host a certain polymer
cluster at the lattice site
\boldsymbol{s}
or a colloid at the lattice site
\boldsymbol{s}+\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}_{x/y/z}
.

B.3
Three dimensions

In the 3D case there are three species of polymer clusters and colloids, their hard interac-
tions are visualized in Fig.
11
(left part). There are six maximal cavities, these are shown
in a reduced form in Fig.
11
(right part). These motivate to start the construction with the following weighted
densities:

\displaystyle m_{1}(\boldsymbol{s})

\displaystyle:=\rho_{\text{pc},x}(\boldsymbol{s})+\rho_{\text{c}}(\boldsymbol{s})

\displaystyle m_{2}(\boldsymbol{s})

\displaystyle:=\rho_{\text{pc},x}(\boldsymbol{s})+\rho_{\text{c}}(\boldsymbol{s}+\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}_{x})

\displaystyle m_{3}(\boldsymbol{s})

\displaystyle:=\rho_{\text{pc},y}(\boldsymbol{s})+\rho_{\text{c}}(\boldsymbol{s})

\displaystyle m_{4}(\boldsymbol{s})

\displaystyle:=\rho_{\text{pc},y}(\boldsymbol{s})+\rho_{\text{c}}(\boldsymbol{s}+\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}_{y})

\displaystyle m_{5}(\boldsymbol{s})

\displaystyle:=\rho_{\text{pc},z}(\boldsymbol{s})+\rho_{\text{c}}(\boldsymbol{s})

\displaystyle m_{6}(\boldsymbol{s})

\displaystyle:=\rho_{\text{pc},z}(\boldsymbol{s})+\rho_{\text{c}}(\boldsymbol{s}+\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}_{z})\qquad.

(79)

A graphical notation of the weighted densities is not particulary helpful. The first ansatz for the free energy functional thus reads

\beta\tilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\text{AO}}^{\text{ex(1)}}\left[\rho_{\text{c}},\,\{\rho_{\text{pc},\alpha}\}\right]=\sum_{\boldsymbol{s}\in\Lambda}\left[\Phi^{(0)}\left(m_{1}(\boldsymbol{s})\right)+\Phi^{(0)}\left(m_{2}(\boldsymbol{s})\right)+\Phi^{(0)}\left(m_{3}(\boldsymbol{s})\right)\right.\\
\left.+\Phi^{(0)}\left(m_{4}(\boldsymbol{s})\right)+\Phi^{(0)}\left(m_{5}(\boldsymbol{s})\right)+\Phi^{(0)}\left(m_{6}(\boldsymbol{s})\right)\right]\qquad.

(80)

Evaluating for the density profiles
\rho_{\text{cav},i}\;(i=1,\ldots,6
) of the 0D cavities of Fig.
11
,
one obtains

\displaystyle\beta\tilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\text{AO}}^{\text{ex(1)}}\left[\rho_{\text{cav},1}\right]

\displaystyle=\Phi^{\text{0D}}\left(m_{1}(\boldsymbol{s})\right)

\displaystyle+\Phi^{\text{0D}}\left(\rho_{\text{pc},x}(\boldsymbol{s})\right)+5\,\Phi^{\text{0D}}\left(\rho_{\text{c}}(\boldsymbol{s})\right)

\displaystyle\beta\tilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\text{AO}}^{\text{ex(1)}}\left[\rho_{\text{cav},2}\right]

\displaystyle=\Phi^{\text{0D}}\left(m_{2}(\boldsymbol{s})\right)

\displaystyle+\Phi^{\text{0D}}\left(\rho_{\text{pc},x}(\boldsymbol{s})\right)+5\,\Phi^{\text{0D}}\left(\rho_{\text{c}}(\boldsymbol{s}+\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}_{x})\right)

\displaystyle\beta\tilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\text{AO}}^{\text{ex(1)}}\left[\rho_{\text{cav},3}\right]

\displaystyle=\Phi^{\text{0D}}\left(m_{3}(\boldsymbol{s})\right)

\displaystyle+\Phi^{\text{0D}}\left(\rho_{\text{pc},y}(\boldsymbol{s})\right)+5\,\Phi^{\text{0D}}\left(\rho_{\text{c}}(\boldsymbol{s})\right)

\displaystyle\beta\tilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\text{AO}}^{\text{ex(1)}}\left[\rho_{\text{cav},4}\right]

\displaystyle=\Phi^{\text{0D}}\left(m_{4}(\boldsymbol{s})\right)

\displaystyle+\Phi^{\text{0D}}\left(\rho_{\text{pc},y}(\boldsymbol{s})\right)+5\,\Phi^{\text{0D}}\left(\rho_{\text{c}}(\boldsymbol{s}+\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}_{y})\right)

\displaystyle\beta\tilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\text{AO}}^{\text{ex(1)}}\left[\rho_{\text{cav},5}\right]

\displaystyle=\Phi^{\text{0D}}\left(m_{5}(\boldsymbol{s})\right)

\displaystyle+\Phi^{\text{0D}}\left(\rho_{\text{pc},z}(\boldsymbol{s})\right)+5\,\Phi^{\text{0D}}\left(\rho_{\text{c}}(\boldsymbol{s})\right)

\displaystyle\beta\tilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\text{AO}}^{\text{ex(1)}}\left[\rho_{\text{cav},6}\right]

\displaystyle=\Phi^{\text{0D}}\left(m_{6}(\boldsymbol{s})\right)

\displaystyle+\Phi^{\text{0D}}\left(\rho_{\text{pc},z}(\boldsymbol{s})\right)+5\,\Phi^{\text{0D}}\left(\rho_{\text{c}}(\boldsymbol{s}+\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}_{z})\right)\qquad.

(81)

Also here spurious contributions in addition to the desired contribution (first term on the rhs, respectively) arise.
An improved functional follows by subtracting these spurious contributions:

\displaystyle\beta\tilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\text{AO}}^{\text{ex(2)}}\left[\rho_{\text{c}},\,\{\rho_{\text{pc},\alpha}\}\right]=\sum_{\boldsymbol{s}\in\Lambda}\Bigl[

\displaystyle\Phi^{\text{0D}}\left(m_{1}(\boldsymbol{s})\right)+\Phi^{\text{0D}}\left(m_{2}(\boldsymbol{s})\right)+\Phi^{\text{0D}}\left(m_{3}(\boldsymbol{s})\right)+\Phi^{\text{0D}}\left(m_{4}(\boldsymbol{s})\right)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\Phi^{\text{0D}}\left(m_{5}(\boldsymbol{s})\right)+\Phi^{\text{0D}}\left(m_{6}(\boldsymbol{s})\right)-\Phi^{\text{0D}}\left(\rho_{\text{pc},x}(\boldsymbol{s})\right)

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\Phi^{\text{0D}}\left(\rho_{\text{pc},y}(\boldsymbol{s})\right)-\Phi^{\text{0D}}\left(\rho_{\text{pc},z}(\boldsymbol{s})\right)-5\,\Phi^{\text{0D}}\left(\rho_{\text{c}}(\boldsymbol{s})\right)\Bigr]\qquad.

(82)

This functional is the final result since the evaluation for the 0D cavity densities
\rho_{\text{cav},i}

gives the exact result
\Phi^{\text{0D}}\left(m_{i}\right)
.
We define the additional weighted densities

\displaystyle m_{7}(\boldsymbol{s}):=

\displaystyle\rho_{\text{pc},x}(\boldsymbol{s})\qquad\qquad m_{8}(\boldsymbol{s})

\displaystyle:=\rho_{\text{pc},y}(\boldsymbol{s})

\displaystyle m_{9}(\boldsymbol{s}):=

\displaystyle\rho_{\text{pc},z}(\boldsymbol{s})\qquad\qquad m_{10}(\boldsymbol{s})

\displaystyle:=\rho_{\text{c}}(\boldsymbol{s})\qquad.

(83)

The excess free energy density in
\beta\tilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\text{AO}}^{\text{ex}}=\sum_{\boldsymbol{s}}\Phi_{\text{AO,3D}}(\boldsymbol{s})
is given by

\Phi_{\text{AO,3D}}(\boldsymbol{s})=\Phi^{\text{0D}}\left(m_{1}\right)+\Phi^{\text{0D}}\left(m_{2}\right)+\Phi^{\text{0D}}\left(m_{3}\right)+\Phi^{\text{0D}}\left(m_{4}\right)+\Phi^{\text{0D}}\left(m_{5}\right)+\Phi^{\text{0D}}\left(m_{6}\right)\\
-\Phi^{\text{0D}}\left(m_{7}\right)-\Phi^{\text{0D}}\left(m_{8}\right)-\Phi^{\text{0D}}\left(m_{9}\right)-5\,\Phi^{\text{0D}}\left(m_{10}\right)\;.

(84)

Appendix C
Surface tensions from DFT

In general, a surface or interface tension is defined in DFT as an excess grand potential
per unit area
\gamma=(\Omega-\Omega_{\text{bulk}})/A
where
\Omega_{\text{bulk}}
is a
bulk grand potential. For a liquid–vapour interface, it is the grand potential of either coexisting
state and for a fluid next to a wall, it is the grand potential of the asymptotic bulk state far away from
the wall.

Figure 12
:
Sketch of different boundary conditions. In figure (a) periodic boundary conditions are chosen. In figure (b) the computational box is connected to a liquid reservoir at the lower end of the box and a vapor reservoir at the upper end. Figure (c) shows a geometry where the system is connected to a bulk reservoir on the left and an infinite potential (wall) constrains the system to the right. In figure (d) two walls constrain the computational box.

Using a box with periodic boundary conditions (and consequently two interfaces, see Fig.
12
(a)), there is no
problem in determining the excess grand potential per unit area as it stands and it
corresponds to twice the surface tension.
For a generic, nonsymmetric situation as depicted in Fig.
12
(b), a peculiar problem arises when
the surface tension is evaluated in a finite computational box which to our knowledge has not been described
before and which is connected to the use of nonsymmetric weight functions in density functionals.
This problem is also apparent when one looks at the grand potential density in the periodic situation: it
is not symmetric for the two interfaces and therefore it is not clear how to evaluate the
surface tension for one interface only.

In order to address that problem,
we assume generically an FMT form for the free energy in which the free energy density is composed
of terms local in densities (i.e. ideal gas and external potential contributions) and other terms
local in weighted densites but nonlocal in densities

\displaystyle\beta\mathcal{F}[\rho_{i}(\boldsymbol{s})]=\sum_{\boldsymbol{s}}\left(\Phi^{\text{loc}}(\rho_{i}(\boldsymbol{s}))+\Phi^{\text{nonloc}}[\rho_{i}(\boldsymbol{s})]\right)\;.

(85)

In our specific case,
\rho_{i}=\{\rho_{\text{c}},\rho_{\text{pc},\alpha}\}
, and there are two types of nonlocal terms.
The first type is in the excess free energy of colloids and polymer clusters and has the form

\Phi^{\text{nonloc}}_{(1),\alpha}(n^{(\alpha)}(\boldsymbol{s}))=\Phi^{\text{0D}}(n^{(\alpha)}(\boldsymbol{s}))
with
n^{(\alpha)}(\boldsymbol{s})=\rho_{\text{pc},\alpha}(\boldsymbol{s})+\rho_{\text{c}}(\boldsymbol{s}+\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}_{\alpha})
(see Eqs. (
72
,
78
,
84
) for the
free energy densities and Eqs. (
67
,
73
,
79
) for the weighted densities).
The second type comes from the subtraction of the colloid one–body term (see Eq. (
35
))
and has the form
\Phi^{\text{nonloc}}_{(2),\alpha}(\boldsymbol{s})=-\rho^{\text{res}}_{\text{p}}\;\rho_{\text{c}}(\boldsymbol{s}+\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}_{\alpha})
.
We need to consider only those terms where the direction
\alpha
is perpendicular to the interface
or wall.

We consider explicitly the situation with the fluid next to a wall (see Fig.
12
(c) for the asymmetric situation and the
corresponding symmetrized one in Fig.
12
(d)) whose resolution also solves
the problem for the liquid-vapor interface. Let
{s}_{0[1]}
be the lower[upper] limit of the one–dimensional computational box.
The upper limit is assumed to be deep in the wall where all densities and free energy densities are zero.
A naive evaluation of the surface tension is given by

\displaystyle\gamma(\mu_{i};[\rho_{j}(\mu_{i})])

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{{s}={s}_{0}}^{{s}_{1}}\left(\Phi^{\text{loc}}(\rho_{i}({s}))-\sum_{i}\mu_{i}\rho_{i}({s}))-\omega_{\text{b}}(\mu_{i})\right)+\sum_{{s}={s}_{0}}^{{s}_{1}}\Phi^{\text{nonloc}}[\rho_{i}({s})]

(86)

where
\mu_{i}
is the bulk chemical potential of species
i
and
\omega_{\text{b}}(\mu_{i})=-p(\mu_{i})
is the grand
potential density of the asymptotic bulk state. Owing to the asymmetry of the grand potential density mentioned above,
this gives a wrong result for
\gamma
. The failure becomes manifest if we consider the Gibbs adsorption
equation

\displaystyle\frac{d\gamma}{d\mu_{i}}=-\Gamma_{i}=-\sum_{{s}={s}_{0}}^{{s}_{1}}(\rho_{i}({s})-\rho_{i,\text{b}})

(87)

(with
\rho_{i,\text{b}}
being the bulk density of species
i
) which should be fulfilled in DFT as can be seen as follows:

\displaystyle\frac{d\gamma}{d\mu_{i}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\gamma}{\partial\mu_{i}}+\sum_{s}\sum_{j}\frac{\partial\gamma}{\partial\rho_{j}({s})}\frac{\partial\rho_{j}({s})}{\partial\mu_{i}}

(88)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\sum_{s}\rho_{i}({s})+\sum_{s}\rho_{i,\text{b}}

(89)

(sums over
{s}
are unrestricted). Here we have used the Gibbs–Duhem relation

\partial p/\partial\mu_{i}=\rho_{i,\text{b}}
and the grand potential minimization condition

\partial\Omega/\partial\rho_{j}({s})=0=\partial\gamma/\partial\rho_{j}({s})
.

However, in the finite box the derivative
\partial\gamma/\partial\rho_{j}({s})
is not zero for
points close to the lower boundary. In our specific case it affects only the derivative with respect to

\rho_{\text{c}}(s_{0})
which should pick up a term from the grand potential density at point
s_{0}-1
(which is absent). Thus
we find

\displaystyle\frac{\partial(\beta\gamma)}{\partial\rho_{\text{c}}({s_{0}})}=-\frac{\partial\Phi^{\text{0D}}(\rho_{\text{c,b}}+\rho_{\text{pc,b}})}{\partial\rho_{\text{c,b}}}+\rho^{\text{res}}_{\text{p}}\;.

(90)

Here we have assumed that at the lower end of the bulk, all densities are at their bulk values. This can be remedied
by adding a term
X
to the surface tension,
\beta\gamma\to\beta\gamma+X
, with the following properties:

\displaystyle\frac{\partial X}{\partial\rho_{\text{c,b}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\Phi^{\text{0D}}(\rho_{\text{c,b}}+\rho_{\text{pc,b}})}{\partial\rho_{\text{c,b}}}-{\rho^{\text{res}}_{\text{p}}}\quad\text{and}

(91)

\displaystyle\frac{\partial X}{\partial\rho_{\text{pc,b}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0\;.

(92)

The term
X
can be constructed from the bulk free energy density
f
which has the properties

\partial f/\partial\rho_{\text{c}}=\mu_{\text{c}}
and

\partial f/\partial\rho_{\text{pc},\alpha}=\partial f/\partial\rho_{\text{pc,b}}=\beta^{-1}\ln\zeta
.
It is given by

\displaystyle X=\frac{1}{2}\Phi^{\text{id}}(\rho_{\text{pc,b}})+\Phi^{\text{0D}}(\rho_{\text{c,b}}+\rho_{\text{pc,b}})-\frac{1}{2}\Phi^{\text{0D}}(\rho_{\text{pc}})-\frac{1}{2}\rho_{\text{pc,b}}\ln\zeta-\rho^{\text{res}}_{\text{p}}\;\rho_{\text{c,b}}\;,

(93)

which is valid in all three dimensions. For the problem of the single
liquid–vapour interface, a second term has to be subtracted (coming from the upper limit of the box)
which has the same form as
X
but differs in the bulk densities (which have to be the ones at the upper limit).
Via this route,
X
is only determined up to a constant but one
can check the new surface tension numerically by comparing to the results in the box with periodic boundary
condition which show that the constant is zero.

The problem with the surface tension for a single surface appears generically in DFT models with nonsymmetric weight
functions, such as e.g. in the lattice rod functionals of Refs.
Oettel2016
;
Mortazavifar2017
.
For symmetric weight functions one can check that missing terms in the derivative
\partial\gamma/\partial\rho_{j}({s})
near the boundaries are cancelled by additional terms coming from derivatives with respect to

\rho_{j}({s})
just outside the box limits. The usual continuum FMT functionals work with symmetric weight functions
such that this problem never arose in previous computations.
